ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e3086	I would like to draw attention to the fact that we will be providing a new service today: The provisional Stenographical Protocol will be published on the session day in a version not yet authorised. That was not the case at the last session, but the minutes were nevertheless put on Facebook and the entry was not deleted by 6 p.m., Mrs Zadić, although You have already been asked to do so in writing by Parliament's Directorate at 11.12 a.m. I should like to ask that all Members of this House proceed correctly. On the new services of the Parliamentary Directorate, in addition to what we now have in addition to additional offers on the website: I think it is an urgent task to inform citizens well and to make public what the working conditions of parliamentarians are. Similarly, it is about making the complex legislative process more transparent and tangible. In this sense, I am allowed to report on the new services provided by Parliament's Directorate. I should like to thank the staff who have set up them very much. First of all, as already mentioned: The provisional Stenographical Protocol is available in an unauthorized version of the Internet from today's meeting. It will also be made available to those who are noted there as intermediaries. This version is not yet authorised, as it is also marked. Interested persons can seek out the various speeches in a timely manner or may then make their speakers' corrections. The trial operation will start today. Secondly, We want to face citizens even closer and more intensively with the legislative process. In this respect, we have set up a new opportunity, as a result of the Committee on Inquiry on “strengthening democracy” in the previous legislative period, in May 2017: Since the beginning of the year, there is the possibility of an online agreement on individual opinions by institutions and individuals. A high number of support from individual opinions will certainly bring not only to the Federal Ministries, but also to Parliamentary operations, to clubs and to Members' suggestions and valuable supplements. In addition, in order to reduce the obstacles to such direct participation, the website offers a brief summary of the content of a legislative project in an understandable way on the overview page of the relevant ministerial draft. Thirdly, In order to speed up the recording of the content of government documents, understandable summaries are offered, short information on the overview page of the submission in question. They then describe the content priorities. Already, the “parliamentary correspondence” offers a comprehensive information service throughout the legislative process. It shall draw up, on all negotiating items, such as government documents, applications and reports, understandable descriptions of the content to be summarised in the main aspects. Fourth: The easier legibility of legislative initiatives is to be illustrated by a new presentation, in particular by textual contrasts. This is designed threefold: The left side contains the current legislative text, the middle column the amendments proposed in the application and the right column the proposed new legislative text by making clear the insertions in red and the deletions in blue. This makes it easy for readers to review proposed changes by means of a single document. The Parliamentary Directorate offers this service not only for new applications, but also retroactively for all the proposals for initiative in the current legislative period. As far as government documents are concerned, a vote is currently taking place with the Federal Chancellery in order to further develop the textual comparisons transmitted by the individual ministries due to the findings in the trial phase. She should never have been, she was never designed, but through bad execution she became exactly that. And that's what corrected this government. Minimum security is now a re-entry aid to the labour market, what it should have been, ladies and gentlemen. This is quite different from an ad hoc ad hoc dish! The Investment Court is an established Court; judges are determined and defined according to clear criteria and ordered for five years. They will also not come together ad hocly, as is always said, to a case and once a time and are not ordered by the companies. What is said is not right! They are appointed a third of Canada, a third of Europe and another third of non-European and non-Canadian representatives, i.e. independents. ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e8412	Madam President. Mrs. Ministra! Ladies and gentlemen. The trade agreement with Canada has been applied provisionally for nine months, and none of the world's downfall prophecies of the NGOs as well as the SPÖ have occurred. They won't come in either. This is, of course, bad for the business model of NGOs. Nevertheless, I would like to force some myths with facts today, because I have also gained the impression in the hearing of the Committee that both Attac and Greenpeace representatives and others have not read or understood a lot. First of all, the precautionary principle, which is alleged to be at risk: This cannot be because this is enshrined in Article 191 of the EU's primary law and cannot be abolished by a international treaty. Canada also uses the precautionary principle in many cases due to its participation in multilateral environmental agreements, and even in national law, the precautionary principle is contained in environmental and sustainability law. But Ceta itself also has important references and references to the precautionary principle. Another criticism was always the rights of workers, but this desire of the left is now fulfilled, because Canada has ratified all eight ILO conventions since June 2017. On the subject of investment protection and regulatory measures, the EU and Canada Parties, in Section D, reaffirm their right – and I quote – to “establish legal political objectives such as the protection of public health, safety, protection of the environment or public morality, social or consumer protection or the promotion and protection of cultural diversity in their respective areas.” Article 8.18 states that an investor can only bring an action against a State if the State violates a duty, i.e. discrimination in the event of expropriation. In addition to this infringement, the investor must still have an actual damage suffered. Article 8.9 states that by amending laws and regulations of a State that has a negative impact on an investor, there is no breach of such an obligation. Thus, the right to regulate, i.e. the state's sovereignty of regulation, is an important part of this agreement. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and FPÖ.]] Furthermore, it is ensured that public services are not undermined by the provision of general services. You will find this not only in the agreement, but also in point 4 of the Joint Declaration, which Mr Klubobmann Kern has attached to the flags and which he was so proud of. I quote: “[...] CETA will not prevent governments from providing public services previously provided by private service providers, or services to which governments have decided to bring them back under public control. ” The wrong paths and the SPÖ zigzag course in trade agreements are known; I would like to quote a few Bonmots at the end of my speech today: There was, on the one hand, an initiator of popular desire, SPÖ mayor Thumpser, who said before running ORF camera that he had read only eight pages of the agreement, then a sentence had come for 15 lines and then he had gone out. Haider: Dilettantic!]] What fascinated me most about the SPÖ survey on trade agreements was the question of whether it really wants standards to be lowered with trade agreements. – In fact, 10 percent have said that they want to, and that really scares me. In Austria, however, this has a massive flaw of beauty: that the top hierarchy is not independent, that is not to be ordered independently, and that there is a doubt of independence. For these, there must be an efficient appeal procedure within the meaning of the rule of law. In Austria, we have the problem that experts have been criticising from all sides, by the UN, by the Council of Europe, for decades, that in the first 24 hours there is a non-independent but rapid commitment. In the first phase, the police themselves are responsible and after that an independent, but not fast, and only in the second stage is the prosecutor's office. That means that there is at no time an efficient and rapid procedure, as planned. Now the People's Prosecution Office is responsible for the prevention of torture. Article 3 of the Convention on Human Rights provides that, in order to prevent torture, the police must be well-trained, the operation must be well planned and there must be an efficient appeal procedure thereafter in the event of complaints. The Council of Europe's Committee on Prevention of Torture has now welcomed the fact that the People's Procuratorate is getting the accusations of abuse on the table. But why does she get these? – In order for it to look at them, and if there are problems with the appeal procedures, it is dedicated to them. What I miss is a recommendation from you for a change of law in the sense of international criticism. What I derive from this for myself is that such cases always give rise to criticism of the public prosecutor's order mode and therefore I will demand a hearing here for NEOS. We know that order mode will not change, but we want a mandatory and transparent hearing in which candidates must prove their competence, their independence and their interest in this subject. This stored information can not only be queryed by remote query, but these stream meters can also be manipulated. We must all work hard to find a solution for this. In Upper Austria alone, we need 700 nurses in the next few years. That's a big number. For example, in my Innviertel constituency Last year, a nursing home with 80 seats was built. However, we can only occupy it with one third of the caregivers because we do not have care staff. We have to make a big change of mind. I think we are in Upper Austria on a very, very good path because there we are the teaching profession of care you want to install. Switzerland is a great model. There are over 4,000 apprentices in Switzerland who learn the profession of care. Interestingly, in Switzerland, the care profession among apprentices is the third most interesting and most popular teaching profession. So we still have a lot of catching up potential. Of course, I would also like to address very briefly the mobile care, which is very important to us, and the 24-hour care. There too we have a great potential up there. But I would also like to say what has been mentioned in the Committee. Indexing the family allowance is not the key to everything, because 75 percent of caregivers have children over 20 years of age, so care due to indexing will certainly not change, not as much as it is reported. I think we must pay particular attention to the training of the care staff. We need to make the profession more attractive, perhaps a little shorter. There are many women who want to enter this care profession because of personal care, but 1 600 hours of training are simply too much for some women. That's where we have to go, but I think we're on a good track. I would like to thank the People's Procuratorate once again for this very, very detailed report. Thank you.

I don't want to say anything about that. The approach may be justified for itself, but in the case of disaster protection – and I cannot say that very strongly enough – I also explained it in the Committee, and I explained it last year and so on and so on – we have a problem. Let's take flood-damaged homeowners: The key word for me was the experience from the large number of complaints, from the case in the Eferdinger Basin, there were 1,500 permanently destroyed family houses. Due to the fact that disaster protection insurance is not liability insurance and because it is not compulsory insurance, the Austrian insurance industry cannot cover reinsurance. The result is that no more than 10 percent – no more than 10 percent! – the building value of insurance can be refunded. That means people stay on 90 percent of the damage. This is not the whole truth, because there are national aid funds, compensation funds and everything possible. On the basis of my basic opinion, however, I believe that: One can put all this on the legs of legal claims instead of making it dependent on the graces of national leaders and so on. I have an example: If an accident with my car occurs tomorrow and my car door is broken, I don't call the head of state and say: I'm sorry, I need a new car door, can't you pay me them out of an aid fund? So, that's the bad approach. I am in favour of the introduction of a system which, due to an existing legal claim, creates a perfect compensation – it will not be perfect because the land remains, it is never payable out, but there can be aid funds to play a role. The introduction of liability insurance for disaster damage should therefore be. This needs some additional measures, but they are easy to implement. There should also be no more construction permits in flood risk zones, red zones – which is currently the case – as these two systems are not comparable to coverage. The red zones are currently not prohibition zones for construction permits. In addition, a ground cover note on flood risk should also be made in the inventory leaflet. Austria is now a country of disaster – from the avalanche in Galtür to the flood in eastern Austria. Now we're going to experience it again: For the people who are now most seriously affected and threatened by floods, it would be the highest railway. We are constantly pushing the problem ahead of us. I have already made efforts at my level to change this, but it also needs Parliamentary consciousness and parliamentary initiative. It is necessary to set up a working group to establish a civil liability insurance system. Other countries already have that. We don't need to reinvent the wheel. Switzerland, Belgium, France – we only need to take these countries as an example and finally create a breakthrough. – I thank you for your attention.

Nevertheless, further improvement measures are important and necessary. The central matura or partialised competence-oriented maturity and diploma exam, as it is precisely called, consists essentially of three pillars. The first pillar is a pre-scientific work, a presentation and a discussion or a diploma thesis at the vocational schools, which is also linked to a presentation and discussion. The second pillar is the interpretation work and the third the oral examination. It is modular, which means that, despite a negative performance in the first or second column, a student can take an oral test – these are the compensation tests. These compensation tests are actually a real chance to improve a not enough. Especially many, we have heard today, namely, Austria's 18 percent, meets this fate in mathematics. Let us look at the example of the rights and obligations of charging point operators: The draft law lays down principles, but these will lead the various countries, that is, our nine federal states, to decide on their own implementing laws. What does this mean in the medium to long term? – We will have our own rules again in each federal state. For operators who want to build a network across Austria, it is of course an extreme administrative effort to make their own applications in each of the federal states and then build up the facilities that are very important to use renewable energy in infrastructure and to use it for vehicles et cetera. What is also exciting is the point of how to regulate that charging points are publicly accessible. There are differences again, since I don't know how to deal with it. It also raises the question of how to do this with legal certainty on this point. In summary, I think, this draft law worked a little too fast, it was a bit shrouded, and in the end something came out of which we have a little abdominal pain. We believe that the direction is the right one, we also believe that these important steps are in the right direction, and will therefore agree with much abdominal pain. However, we would like to note that there are some points where we can still improve. In this sense: Learn a little bit, see that this is done properly, and if you have any questions about it, we will be happy to help you so that this will work a little better next time!
ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e16312	Now Mrs Bundesminister Elisabeth Köstinger has spoken. - Please, Mrs. Minister.
ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e16336	Madam President. Honourable Members to the National Council! I am pleased that we are today able to discuss with you the federal law establishing uniform standards for infrastructure construction for alternative fuels. Especially in transport, CO2 emissions have been rising again since 2016. This also means for us, of course, that we have a great mission to force the attractiveness of clean drives and, of course, to reduce CO2 emissions in transport. This is an essential and major focus, which we are also pursuing with our #mission 2030, our integrated climate and energy strategy. We have set in it the ambitious goal of saving around 7.2 million tonnes of CO2 purely on the market. This is very ambitious, but definitely feasible. Of course, the tank and charging infrastructure will have to make a significant contribution to this. We see that in part we lack the car models – we currently have supply bottlenecks in Austria due to demand – but not only that, but also that will be quite crucial to produce the framework conditions so that the owners of e-cars can really refuel their cars in Austria. This law contributes to the achievement of these objectives, and it also implements an EU regulation. With this law, we create the conditions for charging stations to be publicly accessible. I have to correct Mr Hoyos, who spoke before me, a little bit – he may have referred to an old legislative text –: It is no longer necessary for the regulation of principles and therefore no more national legislation. That was in the first draft, as it was a few months ago, but we have significantly improved. The clarifications lead above all to a significant improvement for users of electric vehicles and, what is important to us, to a closer network of public charging stations. Secondly, the law states that operators of publicly accessible charging points must offer their charging columns to all users, i.e. operators must ensure that their charging points can be used by everyone without, for example, having to provide a membership or similar. At the same time, however, we have introduced useful exceptions, such as e-tax services, e-carsharing models or charging points for e-buses. In this case, an obligation on public access to new investments in charging infrastructure would prevent or make it more difficult. Thirdly, and this is also very important – we all know it from our mobile phone usage – the basis for uniform plug standards at e-loading stations as well as for hydrogen and CNG filling stations is created. The problem that one comes to a gas station and then cannot load because the plug is a wrong one should be eradicated in this area. To this end, standards are adopted by my colleague, Federal Minister Margarete Schramböck, in accordance with EU requirements and thus uniformly implemented the technical standards. This law ensures that both users of electric vehicles and companies have a clear advantage. With reference to his previous speech, I can even give Mr Leichfried an argument so that the SPÖ can also agree – I understand his pain in part, as he, unfortunately, was unable to implement it as a responsible minister at the time –: The accusation that there is no price transparency or any other reason is wrong because the EU directive has already been implemented by national legislation in this area. These are, in fact, the provisions of the pricing and service laws and the appropriateness of the prices by means of provisions of civil law and competition law. These two laws therefore fully meet your demand, i.e. we did not have to do this again because we have already implemented this domestically. On the different methods of pricing – this is already important – and price fixing for publicly accessible charging facilities, however, it is to be said that not only Austria, but also other EU countries have not yet found a uniform business model that has really established itself. We are there in very close exchange with other EU countries, will also observe how this develops and, of course, make adjustments. I hope the SPÖ can also be convinced. There is already this national regulation and implementation, so your points are fully fulfilled. I would be very pleased if we were to have a very broad majority in favour of this law, because it would facilitate our great objective to significantly reduce CO2 emissions. – Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e16570	We're now on the 6th. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. Mr Dominik Schrott has been reported on the floor. Please, honourable Member.
ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e16600	Madam President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! What about the Nagoya Protocol – a term that does not explain itself by itself? After all, it is sometimes also our agricultural groups that have a great economic advantage of placing the relevant products on the market with the help of this genetic stock. So we are talking about a balance. Justice between industrialized countries on the one hand and developing countries on the other. At the same time, however, it is also about stopping the so-called biopiracy, the exploitation of genetic diversity, such as that of the rainforest. This is to be prevented by financial compensation or by the transfer of technologies. Ladies and gentlemen! Austria is the 107, if I have duly reckoned, to Afghanistan and Tanzania. Country that implements this important international provocation for the protection of biodiversity – and this, although we are already on the 23rd. ==References====External links== So from the signature to today, all seven years have passed. Why did it take so long? – The reason for this delay was once again the division of responsibilities between the federal government and countries. (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) There are probably no differences of opinion here in the House that Austria should also support these important concerns. It was a long way to take the actual urgent implementation of this Convention, as has been shown today. We must definitely improve this and react more quickly to such developments, especially on important future issues such as the environment and climate protection. If for years those responsible in this country are not aware of who is responsible for what, how opaque this must be for the citizens! That is why I would like to conclude by stressing the importance of a modern distribution of competences with clear responsibilities. We urgently need clarity in our state and a merger of responsibility for tasks, expenditure and financing, not just in the environment and climate.

ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e17342	Madam President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Federal Law amending the Chemicals Act and the Water Law; Priority: Prohibition of everything that has to do with mercury. In addition to what has already been said: It is based on the Minamata Convention, which is the UN Convention on the Prohibition of Promotion, Trade and Export of Heavy Metal Mercury. This agreement is also the basis for an appropriate EU regulation; a novel to the Chemicals Act will now transfer the requirements – such as import or export restrictions and rules on the management of mercury waste – into Austrian law. We have already heard that mercury is a toxic metal, a heavy metal, which, of course, does not pose significant risks to the environment, to humans, but ultimately also to animals. We know that mercury can cause damage to the nervous system, we also know that it is especially harmful for the unborn. But we also know that many animals are already heavily contaminated today. How does that happen? – The natural way is that over volcanic eruptions, geysers or forest and steppe fires, the mercury is dissolved into the atmosphere, distributed globally and then finally landed again in the rivers or on earth. The problem is: it is completed in the sediment layers. Then, of course, there is also the evil caused by human beings, which means that people have practically caused this mercury load through coal mining for centuries. This also means, of course, that many rivers still suffer from the fact that waste water has been used by industrial plants. Minamata – perhaps a small addition to it – is a Japanese coastal town where, in the fifties of the previous millennium, mercury wastes were led to the sea, 17 000 people were ill, 3,000 died. I particularly welcome the fact that the Federal Government has dedicated itself to mercury and has incorporated it in the government programme, especially in the sense of all living beings, ecology within Austria, but also across the EU and around the world. 40 to 60 percent come to us from outside the EU through emissions. – Thank you for the attention.

Mercury is a heavy metal that is known to be toxic to humans and also to the environment. On the one hand, it is natural in the environment, but on the other hand it is released from the environment, whether by fossil fuels in energy production or in numerous products. In the Minamata Convention, the States have committed themselves to significantly reducing precisely this human-induced release of mercury, which Austria also confesses. In numerous products, such as measuring instruments, lamps and batteries, mercury is still contained in rough quantities. We are going the way to gradually replace the production processes and the products with environmentally compatible alternatives. In order to be able to implement the Minamata Convention now, it is necessary to amend the relevant law. On the comments of Mr Laimer, who claimed that the standard of protection would worsen: The opposite is the case. With this amendment, we will only abolish a number of provisions which are now uniformly resolved on the basis of Europe-wide provisions, so a double burden and a double notification at national level will be unnecessary. ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e18489	Madam President. High house! We experience that climate change is not an abstract, I think, especially this year 2018, in which we first had a really long winter, but then very quickly a hot spring. Especially in recent weeks, we have experienced a massive increase in weather extremes – currently in Neunkirchen, again today, but also in other areas in Lower Austria, in Burgenland, in Styria, in Carinthia – and that hundreds of forces, including many volunteers, are in use to eliminate the damage. This shows that we really need massive action and that climate change in particular is nothing more abstract. We as the Federal Government are dedicated to this issue very intensively, and especially in my department, the issue of combating climate change and, above all, the issue of energy security and energy future have absolute priority. With the #mission 2030, we have created the basis for reducing greenhouse gas emissions by around 36 percent over 2005 in the coming years by 2030. We therefore also commit ourselves to continuing the path taken and to fulfilling the Paris Agreement. The building sector is one of the three largest greenhouse gases-emission sectors in Austria, and in particular our climate and energy strategy is one of the major priorities: this sector accounts for around 27 percent of total Austrian energy consumption. The #mission 2030 has also set itself the goal of raising the recovery rate to an average of 2 percent. This will only be successful if we work together, the Federal Government, countries and communities, to develop a sustainable approach to implementation. We have not only managed to prescribe the path and strategy within 150 days with the integrated climate and energy strategy, but we have started to implement concrete measures from the start of the first day. The present bill provides for the extension of a separate commitment framework for the promotion of thermal rehabilitation measures to buildings in the Environmental Promotion Act in 2019 and 2020. The commitment framework is precisely defined together with the Federal Minister for Finance. In any case, 43.5 million euros are available for the 2018 refurbishment offensive for private and also businesses. Unlike Mr Kollross, the question of thermal rehabilitation has been answered several times by me. We have now decided on the extension and transition to be able to take further action, and we, especially the Federal Government and countries, are working very intensively to reintroduce the area of thermal rehabilitation, because we have seen that the current funding directives are not in practice at all. In recent years, much more has been estimated in the budget than has been collected for thermal renovation. This was partly due to non-practice support provisions, which were too bureaucratic in many areas. This is not going from today to tomorrow, but it really needs an entire concept – as has been said, of course, the countries are involved – and we will present this concept. By 2020, however, we have definitely secured everything. When we get done earlier, of course, there is the opportunity to change and implement it sooner. In any case, we will also fight for the appropriate funds, but that will, of course, also depend on the measures, on how much we will need to do so. With the 2018 Rehabilitation Plan, we are also starting, as has been said, quickly and concretely with the implementation of measures agreed in the Climate and Energy Strategy. What is particularly important to me and what I am particularly pleased about is that we have re-designed the earlier renovation check in terms of content, that we have created a “pure of oil!” bonus. With the new renovation check, a boiler exchange can be promoted away from oil heating to a renewable heating form with up to EUR 5 000. This gives us a strong impetus to get out of the approximately 700 000 oil heaters in Austria step by step, and then move on one step further to achieve our climate and energy goals. Point Report by the Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Protection on the Government Template (151, p._Other Organiser Amendment of the Montreal Protocol on Substances resulting in the degradation of the ozone layer (158 d.a.)
ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e18896	We're on our way to the 10th. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. First of all, Mr Deputies is a jewelry beater. - Please, ladies and gentlemen.
ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e18923	Madam President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen, The Montreal Protocol ultimately determines the degradation, reduction and prohibition of the ozone layer destructive substances such as fluorochlorocarbons. This has already been recognized in the 1980s, implemented in this Protocol and implemented by The European Union is a member of the European Union. However, it was necessary to observe that these substances were replaced by so-called semi-halogenated fluorocarbons. This change is also about gradually pushing and prohibiting these substances from the market, because they are less greenhouse gas effective than fluorochlorocarbons, but still a thousand times more harmful than carbon dioxide, for example. This is an important step towards achieving the two-degree goal of the Paris climate agreement. With the ban on these substances, we have the potential to account for up to 2100 the total global warming by 0.5 degrees Celsius. I believe that this is very, very important, so it has come to a unanimous agreement in the Committee on Economic and Monetary Affairs and Industrial Policy on these amendments, and there is a great deal of agreement here too. One important point for me, however, is also to point out that a multilateral fund has already been set up in the 1990s, which also allows so-called developing countries to withdraw from the use of these products, in a somewhat time-consuming version, but is supported by the industrialized countries, because climate policy cannot be stopped at the borders. Despite the incredible efforts we are making in Europe, which we are now implementing nationally with the #mission 2030, in the face of a climate and energy strategy that can really be seen, of course, we need international cohesion in order to maintain the global climate and ultimately reduce climate disasters. I would therefore like to thank you very much for the overall agreement in the Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Protection.

ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e19056	Madam President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! High house! In the last Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Protection, we have discussed an important change in the Montreal Protocol on the protection of the ozone layer. In future, so-called semi-halogenated hydrocarbons, in short HFCs, will also be regulated in the Montreal Protocol. Following the ban of the FCFC, which was used until then as a fuel in spray cans, foams and as a refrigerant in refrigerators and air conditioning, the replacement was found in HFCs. Although these are not as strong ozone killers as their predecessors, unfortunately they contribute significantly to climate warming, as according to environmental organisations they are much more harmful than CO2, which has already been reported by my previous speakers. Therefore, it is now high time to reduce even these HFCs. The effect of such multilateral agreements shows the success of the 1987 Montreal Protocol. After the global exit from FCK 2015 has been completed, first positive results have now been shown: The concentration of cause substances in the lower atmosphere has actually decreased. The reduction obligations for HFCs relate to both production and consumption. HFCs are to be reduced by 85 percent by 2036. For industry, this changeover to more environmentally friendly substances should not be a major problem. Propane, for example, is obtained in the promotion of natural gas as a by-product and can therefore be produced in Austria, which would in turn benefit our economy. When voting in the Environment Committee, it was unanimously decided to recommend to the National Council the approval of the conclusion of this State Treaty. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e20344	We're on the 12th. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. Mr Lindinger is speaking on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats). Please, honourable Member.
ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e20372	Madam President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! Appreciate Seers and Advisors! Ladies and gentlemen here in the House! We are discussing here the Federal Law on measures to protect plants against plant pests, in short the Plant Protection Act. I would like to point out that this is not to be confused with the Plant Protection Act. The first phytosanitary law was adopted in 2007. This law regulates the protection of plants and plant products from harmful organisms and also the control of harmful organisms. What are the concrete changes and objectives of the draft law presented? – It is about improving phytosanitary safety, i.e. phytosanitary health, it is about rapid measures in case of pests, it is also about implementing EU rules, it is about increasing the efficiency in official controls, and it is about preventing the introduction and spread of dangerous plant pests. In Austria, we have very strict controls, and that is exactly how there must be strict controls on imports into the European market. I also support the extension of mandatory plant health certificates. This enables us to control all trade flows between companies. Accompanying measures under this law will also include adaptations due to climate change. That must also be a general concern for us, and that is it. I should like to make this clear to you, ladies and gentlemen, on the basis of an example: The bacterium Xylella fastidiosa – or, as we call it, fire bacterium – can be spread through small insects and can also cause diseases in many crops. Originally, it was a bacterium widespread in the USA, and it was probably brought to Europe by infected coffee plants in 2012/13. In 2013 it was first found in southern Italy, and since 2013, more than a million olive trees have fallen victim to this bacterium in southern Italy. What makes me stupid is that this pathogen can also attack fruit trees and vines. In a few years, domestic trees could also be in danger. The progression of the disease is reinforced by rising temperatures and climate change, and this law is intended to prevent imports. Therefore, measures relating to climate change are essential, and increased controls and stricter import rules will improve domestic plant protection. This also needs the support of the new and efficient management systems. Policy must define the framework conditions, and this law is the foundation for modernising the control of feed and food and for an intact plant health in Austria. This law serves to protect feed here in Austria, it serves to protect food here in Austria, and it ultimately also serves to protect the population here in Austria. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e20708	Madam President. Honourable Members! I can also take the opportunity here to briefly draw a few points on what it really is about when adopting the Plant Protection Act 2018. The basis is a change in EU law that we implement. There are accompanying measures on the new plant pest regulation and on the new Regulation on official controls of the European Union. In addition to the adoption of accompanying rules such as those relating to authority competence, the requirements for control bodies and also the definition of data protection rules and criminal provisions, it is necessary to implement some specific objectives of the new legislation. We extend the control obligation on pests to all commercial trade flows. This concerns both production and trade in plants and plant products. The strengthening of the import rules will effectively prevent imports from third countries and the further spread of dangerous pests, and the exemptions for travel imports will be greatly reduced. The second objective we are pursuing with the regulation is to increase the efficiency of controls. This means that through measures such as the harmonisation of official controls in all areas of food safety at European level and the introduction of quality management systems by the control authorities, we want and will also achieve this efficiency improvement. I would be delighted to have a very broad consent to the Plant Protection Act 2018. – Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e20741	Thank you very much, Mrs. Minister. Mrs Krenn is now speaking. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e20768	Madam President. Appreciate Mrs Minister! Dear spectators at the gallery! Dear spectators in front of the TV screens! (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) Due to globalising trade and climate change, there is an increased risk of spreading dangerous plant pests. Our native plant world is under threat. Yes, the threat scenario is real. Current hazards are caused by the Japanese eskastania gallwespe, the Asian deciduous beetles, fungal pests dangerous oaks and beech. At present, we in Central Europe have caused a very massive death of ash, caused by mushrooms. The American Rebbekade threatens the vines, the Kirschessyfly threatens fruitwoods. What is the main objective of this Plant Protection Act? – It includes the extension of the plant passport requirement for all B2B trade flows, the strengthening of import controls, harmonised monitoring and in the event of an infestation. In this sense, this phytosanitary law is very important, it is very, very important that it be intensified and that something is finally happening. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and representatives of the FPÖ.]] It is important for us, for our environment, but above all for our farmers. ParlaMint-AT_2018-06-13-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00028_d7e20834	Madam President. Thank you, Mrs. Minister! High house! Ladies and gentlemen in the gallery and in front of the screens at home! For me, agriculture and forestry is a matter of my heart, especially since in my village of Thaua in the municipality of Allensteig, which is located in the center of the forest district, there are still many agricultural and forestry farms. Of course, I would like to see you all, as likely, that our domestic agricultural and forestry farms are best protected from dangerous plant pests. By adopting this Plant Protection Act, we implement European plant protection provisions aimed at improving phytosanitary safety, preventing the introduction or spread of hazardous plant pests and increasing the efficiency of official controls. As a result of globalised trade and climate change, there is an ever-increasing risk that dangerous plant pests will be introduced and spread in Austria. It is therefore only to be welcomed that the rules on plant health are tightened. Plant health is important not only for plant production, but also for forests, natural areas as well as cultural areas, ecosystems and biodiversity. In order to ensure plant health there will be an extension of the plant passport requirement for all B2B trade flows, and official controls will also be made more effective. In addition, the Plant Protection Act lays down measures to be taken immediately if plant pests have already spread in an area. Ladies and gentlemen, by doing so we can ensure that our native plants are best protected from pests. This will ultimately benefit our domestic farms, but also we as consumers, because agriculture can provide sufficient quality food thanks to modern plant protection. Thank you.

The other story, which is not less exciting, is that these invasive plants behave quite normally in their country of origin, because they are also in competition there.
